  5. *Export pressed.* `exportNow` does nothing while Export is `aria-disabled`, while a run is on, or after an export that ended with a note. It clears the alert line and the status line, builds the job `{database, tables: [{name, count}], plan}` from the ticked rows in the dialog's order, swaps the dialog to its running state (the list, the line and Export are hidden; Cancel has the focus) and calls `onExport(job, {progress, cancelled})`. `run` answers with an outcome whatever happens; should its promise reject all the same, the alert line says `EXPORT_TABLES_FAILED_MESSAGE`.
  6. *`run`.* It refuses with a message (shown on the dialog's alert line, nothing started) when it is already running, when a database is loading (`EXPORT_TABLES_LOADING_DIALOG_MESSAGE`), when an import runs, or when `getDatabase()` is not `job.database` (`EXPORT_TABLES_GONE_MESSAGE`): the dialog's own checks again, the second lock. Then `onBusy(true)` (cleared in the `finally`, step 9), one `nextFrame()` (`import-pace.js`), and a look at Cancel. When the plan needs a library, `progress({library: true})` shows "Loading the zip tool…" and `loadVendor` is raced against a look at `cancelled()` every `CANCEL_LOOK_MS` (100 ms), so the busy state never outlives a closed dialog by more than that; a library that cannot be loaded gives `EXPORT_TABLES_LIBRARY_FAILED_MESSAGE` with every tick kept (Export can be pressed again, and the loader tries again).
  7. *The sink.* The bytes of the file go to a sink: `begin(name)` starts an entry, `write(bytes)` adds to it, `end()` closes it, `problem()` gives an error when the output cannot go on (else null) and `finish()` gives the one `Blob`. Nothing in a sink knows CSV, but what it is given is CSV bytes (step 8). `fileSink(type)` keeps the byte arrays in a list: one entry, and no limit of its own. `zipSink(fflate, type, entries, limits?)` wraps an fflate `Zip` whose callback copies each chunk (`chunk.slice()`: the library's pass-through hands back the buffer it was given) and keeps the first error; `begin(name)` starts a `ZipDeflate` entry at `EXPORT_ZIP_LEVEL` (6) with `mtime` set as a property of the entry before `zip.add` (an option of that name is ignored). The date is `zipDate()`: the clock, held to the years 1980 to 2099 that a zip can date (a clock before 1980 gives 1 January 1980, one after 2099 gives 31 December 2099); the library refuses any other year, and a computer whose clock is far off would otherwise get no zip. The zip's `problem()` is the library's first error (`EXPORT_TABLES_ZIP_FAILED_MESSAGE`) or `zipProblem` over `Math.max(count, entries)` and the largest of the zip's size so far, the largest entry closed and the entry now open (an entry's sizes are 32 bits as well); `entries` is how many entries the zip will be given. `run` makes its sink in one way only, `sinkFor(plan, zipTool, limits?)`: a file's sink for a plan of kind `file`, a zip's for a plan of kind `zip`, told how many entries the plan has, and a `TypeError` that names the kind (`unknown kind of output: …`) for any other: `planOutput` makes no other today, and a later phase adds its sink there. It asks the sink's `problem()` at once and throws what the sink says. So a database of more tables than a zip holds is refused before any table is read (`EXPORT_TABLES_ZIP_TOO_BIG_MESSAGE`), and a test holds that in Node with small limits, as it holds `zipSink`. That `run` makes its sink through `sinkFor` is read, and no test holds it: a test through the page would need a database of more than 65,535 tables, and the engine alone takes a minute and a half to make that many. The tests hold `sinkFor` itself. It was seen once by hand: with 65,536 tables, all ticked, Export answers with the words of a zip that is too big and no table is opened to be read (in Chromium and in Firefox), and with 65,535 the zip is made and each table is opened once (in Chromium only). `finish()` ends the zip and asks once more.
  8. *The pieces.* For each table: `openRows`; every entry starts with `CSV_BOM` and `headerText(reader.columns)`; then pieces: `reader.next({chars: EXPORT_PIECE_CHARS, ms: EXPORT_PIECE_MS})` (a piece ends once it has reached 65,536 characters of values or 25 ms, and holds at least one row; the row that reaches the budget is taken, so a piece can pass it by one row), turned into one text by `rowText`, encoded as UTF-8 and written to the sink. It is `run` itself that makes the bytes (the byte-order mark, the header, `rowText`, the encoding): the sinks only keep them. After every piece, the last too: the sink is asked for a problem (a zip error, or `zipProblem`, as step 7 says), `progress({at, of, name, rows, count, doneRows, allRows})` is called, `await pace.rest()` and Cancel is looked at. The reader is closed after the loop over a table's pieces has ended, and in the `finally` of `run` if the loop was left earlier (Cancel, a failure).
  9. *The end.* When every table has been read to its end and Cancel has been looked at once more: the zip is closed (`zip.end()`, then its problem again), the sink gives one `Blob`, and `saveFile(blob, plan.fileName, plan.type)` (`export.js`) hands it to the browser. Only then is the event recorded, then the toast shown, then `{ok: true, fileName, written: [{name, rows, entry}], notes}` returned (`notes` holds `{kind: "blob", tables}` when some table held a BLOB, else is empty). The event is `recordEvent(runLog.current().key, {kind: "export", lines})`: the first line `file: <file name>, <n> bytes` (`exportEventFileLine`), then one line for each table in order, `table: <table>, <n> rows` for a single CSV or `table: <table>, <n> rows, <entry name>` for a zip (`exportEventTableLine`). The numbers are the rows written, never the counts shown before (a view can give other rows than it counted), and the bytes are the Blob's size. If `saveFile` throws, nothing is recorded and the message is `EXPORT_TABLES_SAVE_FAILED_MESSAGE`. From the moment `saveFile` has returned, nothing can turn the export into a failed one: the event and the toast are tried in a `try` of their own, and what they throw goes to the console only (`console.debug`), so the student is never told that nothing was exported beside a file that is on the disk. The `finally` of `run` closes the reader (a throw from `close()` is caught) and, in a `finally` of its own inside that one, clears `running` and calls `onBusy(false)`, so that the busy state ends even when closing the reader fails. An export is not a run: nothing goes into the run log or the History tab, the status bar keeps the last query's line, and no page event is sent (the database is not changed, so there is no `lab:database-changed`).
  10. *The dialog again.* First the status line is emptied and the count of tables announced is reset, as step 5 did when the export began: a retry names its first table again, and nothing stale is left in the page. On `{ok: true}` with no note it closes (`dialog.close()`, the focus goes back by the rule below). With a note it stays: the note is found among `outcome.notes` by its kind (`kind === "blob"`), the list, the hold line and Export are hidden, and so is the line of words under the title (`#export-tables-text`, which stays while the export runs, as the import's does), `#export-tables-note` holds `exportTablesBlobNote(fileName, tables)` (one paragraph that begins with the "Exported …" sentence, because a toast under a modal dialog is not heard, and names the tables), Cancel becomes Close (the same button, `#export-tables-cancel`) and has the focus, the dialog's `aria-describedby` and Close's point to the note, and the toast is shown as well. On `{ok: false}` with a message the list comes back with the message on the alert line (`#export-tables-error`, `role="alert"`) and the focus on Cancel; with a `table` (it could not be read to its end) that row says `exportTablesUnreadText(reason)`, the engine's words included, and goes on saying it after the next Export, and it cannot be ticked any more; the other ticks stay. On `{ok: false, cancelled: true}` the dialog has closed long before and does not look at the answer.
- **Cancel.** Cancel, Escape and any other way the dialog closes while it runs close it at once (`endSession`: the session counter moves on and the run's `cancelled()` turns true); the run notices at its next look (after `nextFrame`, after the library, after a table is opened, after each piece, and before the file is saved), saves nothing, records nothing, closes its reader, clears the busy state and says so in a note toast (`EXPORT_TABLES_CANCELLED_MESSAGE`). Escape cannot be held back in a `<dialog>`, as for the import. Escape is told to the run at once: the dialog's own `cancel` event, which the browser sends before it closes the dialog (its `close` event comes only later), ends the session, and so sets the same stop flag, and closes a window that `close` leaves: a step of the export that was already waiting for its turn when Escape came would see no Cancel, and could save a file for a dialog that has gone. What it does not cover: a key or a press that reaches the page only after the last piece has been written is too late, the file is saved, and the toast and the event say so (the last look at Cancel is before `saveFile`, with no wait between the two, so the button cannot land in between; only a key that the browser hands over late can). In Firefox under test, a key pressed while the page was busy was handed over only after the export's next step, whatever the page listened to (`cancel` and `keydown` both), so there the window is a whole step wide. **A held key.** A key held down repeats: a repeated Enter (`event.repeat`) does nothing in this dialog (a `keydown` listener cancels it), because Enter held on the menu entry would press Export in a dialog that has only just opened, and Enter held on Export would press Cancel, which has the focus once the export runs. It does nothing once the dialog has closed under the key either: `endSession` calls `swallowHeldEnter()` (`held-enter.js`, 3.2), once for each closing, the first time the dialog hears of it (for Escape, at the `cancel` event), so that whichever way the dialog closes, the repeats that follow press nothing, wherever the focus is, until the key is let go. Without it, Enter held on Export with a small table (it is exported before the key is let go), or on Cancel, or on the note's Close, sent the focus back to the Export button; the next repeat opened the Export menu, the next pressed "Database (.db)…", and that dialog saved the database file again and again. The import's dialog does the same (9.6). What it does not cover: Enter held on the Export button itself, with no dialog of a long job in between, still opens the menu and goes on into "Database (.db)…"; that is older than the plan and left as it was, so that the page with every flag off is unchanged. Until the run has noticed, the export is still busy, and the menu entry answers with `EXPORT_TABLES_BUSY_MESSAGE` and no new export starts.
- **Errors.** What the student reads depends on what failed. A table that the engine cannot read to its end (it could not be opened, or `step()` threw, as a view can that counted well) stops the whole export: nothing is saved, no event is recorded, the alert line says `exportTablesFailedText(table, reason)` ("Nothing was exported, because v_bad could not be read. The database said: integer overflow"), and the row of that table says `exportTablesUnreadText(reason)` and cannot be ticked again. Only an error of the engine names a table (`isEngineError`: its `SQLite3Error` and its allocator's `WasmAllocError`), and one kind of it does not: an engine that ran out of memory (`isOutOfMemory`, which also holds for the reader's own guard when the engine gives no address for a text) is not that table's failure, since another try, or fewer tables, can go well. It is told as the browser's memory, `EXPORT_TABLES_OUT_OF_MEMORY_MESSAGE` ("The browser ran out of memory, and nothing was exported."); no table is named, and the row is not closed: it keeps its tick and can be ticked again. For the others, `reason` is `engineLine` of the error, and is in the outcome beside `table`. An error of the page's own code while rows are read or turned into text (`rowText`) gives `EXPORT_TABLES_FAILED_MESSAGE`, or `EXPORT_TABLES_OUT_OF_MEMORY_MESSAGE` when it is a `RangeError` (a text or an array too large: `Invalid string length`, `Array buffer allocation failed`) or Firefox's `InternalError`. While the file is made (the header and the pieces encoded and written to the sink, the zip, the `Blob`) a failure says `EXPORT_TABLES_FILE_FAILED_MESSAGE` for a single CSV and `EXPORT_TABLES_ZIP_FAILED_MESSAGE` for a zip (the library reported an error, or `add` or a push threw), and the memory message for a `RangeError`, an engine that had no memory and a `WasmAllocError`. A zip that would pass what a zip without Zip64 holds (65,535 entries, or 4 GiB: `EXPORT_ZIP_MAX_ENTRIES` is 0xffff and `EXPORT_ZIP_MAX_BYTES` 0xffffffff; the library writes wrong numbers there without an error) gives `EXPORT_TABLES_ZIP_TOO_BIG_MESSAGE`, before any table is read when the number of tables alone is too many. A library that cannot be loaded gives `EXPORT_TABLES_LIBRARY_FAILED_MESSAGE`, a browser that refuses the file `EXPORT_TABLES_SAVE_FAILED_MESSAGE`. What is behind a message goes to the console only (`console.debug`). In every case the reader is closed and the busy state ends (the `finally`, step 9); after `saveFile` nothing is reported as a failure at all (step 9).
- **The busy state and the page** (`index.js`). `runningJob` ("import", "export" or null), `jobMessage` (the running job's own busy message) and `addressAtJob` (the address when it began) are declared before both mounts, because a closure that reads a `let` before its declaration throws, and are written by the `onBusy` of both jobs (`onJobBusy(kind, message)`). They replaced the import's own `addressAtImport` and the reads of `importer.busy()`. `jobRunning()` is true while either runs; `otherJobMessage(kind)` gives the message of a running job that is not `kind`, and each job is handed that of the other: `longJob(kind, message)` gives a mount its `onBusy` and its `otherJobMessage` together (`...longJob("export", EXPORT_TABLES_BUSY_MESSAGE)` for the export, `...longJob("import", IMPORT_BUSY_MESSAGE)` for the import), so an export is never started during an import and an import never during an export (`take()` and `run()` of `import.js` refuse with it; the default `() => null` keeps every existing test as it was). `refusedWhileBusy()` replaced `refusedWhileImporting()`: it toasts the running job's own words and answers whether the caller must stop. While an export runs, therefore, these are refused with `EXPORT_TABLES_BUSY_MESSAGE`, as for an import (9.6): Run and a Check (`isLoading` of the exercise panel includes `jobRunning()`; a Run that was waiting its frame is dropped), the database export (`getProblem`, and again in `onExport`), a pick in the switcher (put back with `picker.sync()`), "Open a file…", Ctrl+O, Open from URL and Ctrl+U, opening or closing an exercise set (`lab.syncPicker()`), and a file chosen (a file dropped is not answered with the message: it is ignored without a word, as under any open dialog, and with the import flag off a drop is not taken at all); Back and Forward cannot be refused, and `popstate` puts the address back (`pushState` of `addressAtJob`) with the running job's toast. `jobInTheWay()` (replacing `importInTheWay()`) is the same and also true while a dialog is open, whichever it is (it asks the page for `dialog[open]`, as the drop does), for Ctrl+O and Ctrl+U under a modal dialog: until the features were switched on it asked only the import's and the export's dialogs, and under the evidence dialog, the database export dialog or the Open from URL dialog the file chooser still opened, so that a CSV file chosen there opened the import's dialog on top of the first (`tests/ui4-review.spec.js`). In the Open from URL dialog itself Ctrl+U goes to the address field: `openUrl` asks `openUrlDialog.isOpen()` before `jobInTheWay()`. What is refused is the key, not the file: a file that arrives from a chooser that was already open when a dialog opened is taken as ever (the `change` of `#open-file-input` asks nothing of the dialogs), and so is a database that Back or Forward opens. A person cannot use the page while the browser's chooser is open, and each dialog is safe against what happens under it: the import's dialog takes a second choice as a new one; the import's dialog, the database export's, the export of tables' and the evidence dialog each close when another database loads (the Open from URL dialog stays, with what is typed in it); the export of tables looks at the page again when Export is pressed, since an import can have begun above it; and the evidence dialog reads the open database's log when it exports. A database that opens under the idle export dialog (Back or Forward) closes it (`lab:database-loaded`), as for the import's. The export's controller has no `busy()` to ask (the page is told by `onBusy`); `importer.busy()` of the import stays: tests call it.
- **The decisions the plan left open, as built, with their reasons.**
  - *What is listed.* The tables and views of `main` whose names do not start with `sqlite_`, in SQL's own `ORDER BY name` (by bytes: a sort in JavaScript compares UTF-16 units and would put some names elsewhere, and the plan says `sales/2024` comes before `sales_2024`). TEMP objects are not listed: they belong to the connection (the import's staging table is one) and are not the student's database. A virtual table and its shadow tables are listed like any table. Every statement names the schema, `main."name"`: a TEMP table of the same name hides a main one otherwise, and the export would write the wrong table under the right name.
  - *Nothing is ticked at the start*, except that when the list has exactly one object at the moment the dialog opens it is ticked, since there is nothing to choose; no tick is ever set by a count or a size. What goes into a file for marking is the student's choice, and a preset "all" would put in tables they never looked at. With two or more rows there is one more tick box above the list, "All tables and views".
  - *Counting, one at a time, with a rest between* (plan section 5), and the dialog is on screen before the first count, so the student sees it open at once. One slow `count(*)` cannot be interrupted (below).
  - *The line beside Export* (`#export-tables-hold`, a permanent `role="status"` element whose text is set, never inserted: a live region that is inserted with its text is often not announced). While counts are pending it says `EXPORT_TABLES_HOLD_COUNTING`; once counting has ended, with no row that can be ticked (every count failed) it says `EXPORT_TABLES_HOLD_NOTHING` (a line that asked for a tick would ask for what cannot be done), with nothing ticked `EXPORT_TABLES_HOLD_NONE`, else what Export will save (`exportTablesWillSaveText`). The line is written only when its words change, and so is the line of a row (`row.shown`): a status is told again each time it is written, the same words included, and a long list is not built again for one tick. *Export is `aria-disabled`, not `disabled`*, while counting is on and while nothing is ticked: it keeps its place in the Tab order, a press does nothing, and `aria-describedby` ties it to the line above, so a keyboard or screen reader user meets the reason; the import's dialog uses `disabled` and says why beside it. `css/export-tables.css` draws it as a plain disabled button (the attribute does not get `buttons.css`'s disabled look).
  - *A row that cannot be ticked* (its count failed, or it could not be read) has `aria-disabled="true"` on its tick box instead of `disabled`, for the same reason (its reason is read), and a press leaves it unticked. The box for all is the same when no row can be ticked. Such a box is dimmed (`opacity: 0.4`) and so is the name beside it (`--graphite`; in forced colours `GrayText`, as Export is when it is off), because the browser draws an `aria-disabled` box like any other.
  - *File names.* A single CSV: `sanitiseName(table)` (`import-rules.js`: letters, digits and underscores, anything else one underscore, `c_` in front of a leading digit), `table` when nothing is left, cut to `EXPORT_NAME_MAX_CHARS` (100) characters, then `.csv` (a name over 255 bytes cannot be kept by a computer or taken out of a zip, and a character of a sanitised name is a byte); it is not `tableNameFor`, which cuts at the last dot as a file's name has an extension, so a table `data.v2` stays `data_v2.csv`. Several: the same, made unique among the ticked tables, after the cut, by the rule of `uniqueName`, judged with `sameNameKey` (`csvFileNames` counts for itself and goes on where it stopped; two tables whose names are 300 n's give a name of 100 n's and `.csv`, and the same with `_2`; `_2`, `_3`, judged without regard to the case of A to Z, as the import judges duplicates), worked out again whenever the ticks change and in the dialog's order. So `sales_2024` exported alone is `sales_2024.csv`, and `sales_2024_2.csv` only when `sales/2024`, which comes first, is exported with it; two names that both sanitise to `_` give `_.csv` and `__2.csv` (what the import does with the same names). The zip: `safeBaseName(database.name)` (`export.js`, which has already removed `.sql`) without a `.db`, `.sqlite` or `.sqlite3` at its end (any letter case), then `-tables.zip`: the database's name, not the label the switcher shows, as the results download names its file (`csvFileName`, `results.js`). `con.csv` is left as it is (Q24): Windows cannot create a file of that name, and the plan says to leave it.
  - *A row says its file name* ("Saved as sales_2024_2.csv") only when it is ticked and its entry is `renamed` (its name is not `<table name>.csv`), so a table called `Order Details` says "Saved as Order_Details.csv" and `sales/2024` alone says "Saved as sales_2024.csv".
  - *The library is asked for only for a zip* (two or more tables): one table needs none, and the page's first request is the first export of two. Zip entries are deflated at level 6 and dated by the clock at the time of the export, held to the years 1980 to 2099 (step 7).
  - *Pieces and the pace.* A piece ends once it has reached 65,536 characters of values or 25 ms, and holds at least one row (a row of one huge text is one piece; the row that reaches the budget is still taken), so that the page gets a turn and Cancel is heard whatever the table's size; after every piece, the last too, the page rests (`pace.rest()`, with a drawn frame every `PAINT_EVERY_MS`, so that the progress bar moves) and Cancel is looked at.
  - *Event.* One event for each file that was saved (not one for each table), under `runLog.current().key`, after `saveFile` has returned; an event that is cancelled, that failed, or whose file the browser would not take is never recorded. An event holds 200 lines of 500 characters (9.5): with a database of 200 tables or more in one zip the event lists the file and the first 198 tables and its last line says `(N more lines are not listed)`, N being the tables left out; a table's name of 486 characters or more (380 in a zip, where the line is `table: <name>, <n> rows, <entry>` and holds the entry's name too) loses the end of its line (9.5 has the numbers). Up to 199 tables are listed in full. A table's name with a line break, or another control character, shows on one line with the break turned into `_`, as every name taken from a file does (`sanitizeLine`): the event's name is then not exactly the table's.
  - *The end of an export.* Nothing to add: the dialog closes, the focus goes back where it came from, the toast says it. Something to add (in this phase: a BLOB): the note, as above. The notice for events that could not be stored names what this visit can note down (`storageNoticeEventsText`, 3.2), because the log's events are no longer only imports.
  - *Number rules the plan left open* (its section 9; they are now in section 5 and in "What comes back" there). A REAL `-0.0` is written `-0.0`, and an infinite REAL `Infinity` or `-Infinity`, the word the results pane shows (no digits can write it). A table can show less than that: in a column declared REAL the engine itself keeps a minus zero as 0, so the file says `0.0` there, and `-0.0` is written only where the engine hands it over, in a column without that type or from a view. Their effects on a re-import are known and kept: a column that holds an infinity comes back as TEXT (`Infinity` is not a plain number to the import), and `-0.0` comes back as 0.0 (the import stores a decimal that is minus zero as 0.0, 9.6). `tests/fixtures/tools/rules.py` was given the BLOB rule that the plan's section 10 asks for and one line for infinity on Nov 17, 2026 (negative zero needed none: it follows from how Python writes the number), the two scripts that make the fixtures were changed with it (`gen_export.py` and `gen_expected.py`: `mixed_types` among the tables that have an expected file, and the wording of Q2 and Q22), and they were run again, which wrote `mixed_types.csv` and wrote `expected/export.json`, `expected/open_questions.json` and the README's rows for Q2 and Q22 again.
  - *Focus.* Open: the first tick box. Running: Cancel. An error: Cancel. The note: Close. Closed: `returnFocus` (the Export button, or More on a phone), by the same rule as the other dialogs: only when the focus is on the body or inside the dialog, so that a student who has moved elsewhere is not pulled back.
- **The dialog's shape** (`css/export-tables.css`). A native `<dialog id="export-tables-dialog" class="app-dialog tables-dialog">` built with `createElement` and `textContent` only (a table's name comes from a database), in two parts like the import's: `.tables-body`, which scrolls (the title `#export-tables-title`, the line `#export-tables-text`, the "All tables and views" box `#export-tables-all`, the list `ul.tables-list` (it says `role="list"`, as the exercise lists do: some browsers drop the role of a list that shows no markers), the progress, the note), and under it `.tables-foot`, which does not (`#export-tables-hold`, `#export-tables-error`, Export `#export-tables-submit`, Cancel `#export-tables-cancel`). `.tables-body` is `position: relative`: the hidden status line (`.visually-hidden`, which is taken out of the flow) is then placed by what scrolls; placed by the dialog, it lay as far down as the list is long, and in Chromium the dialog itself then scrolled with a long list, title and buttons with it. It is not the sticky bar of the import's first build (9.6 says why not). A row is a real `<input type="checkbox">` with a `<label>` that holds only the name (as text, whatever it holds) and the word "view" for a view, and a separate paragraph, tied to the tick box by `aria-describedby`, with the count, "Counting rows…", the reason, and "Saved as …"; names wrap (`overflow-wrap: anywhere`), are never cut and have no `title`; the tick box is level with the first line of the name in every row (`align-self: start` and a `margin-top` of half of what a line of the name is taller than the box; the box is given the name's text size and the row's line height, and the margin is measured in lines, `1lh`, so that it holds too when the browser draws text larger than the page asks, as a minimum text size in its settings does), and a row of one line has that line in the middle of its height (`align-content: center`); under 36rem the row's line goes under its name; wider than that, what is beside the name never takes more than half of the row (`fit-content(50%)`), so that a long reason or a long "Saved as …" leaves the name the other half and both wrap; and the line of a row that cannot be ticked, which is a sentence, goes under its name at every width. While it runs, the progress block (`aria-busy` on that block only, never on the dialog) holds a heading ("Exporting table 2 of 5: orders", "Exporting basic" for one table, "Loading the zip tool…" while the library is awaited), a second line ("1,200 of 2,000 rows", "0 of 1 row", and without the "of" when more rows come than were counted) and a `<progress>` named by the heading whose value is the rows written over the rows counted, at most 1; one polite status line outside the block says the heading when the table changes, never a stream of numbers (it is emptied, with the count of tables announced, when an export starts and when it ends). It uses the shared `.app-dialog-*` classes of `css/evidence.css`, the `results-loading-*` and `results-progress` classes of `css/result.css` for the progress (as the import does) and the tokens only, and none of the `.import-*` classes.
- **Limits, and what is not decided.**
  - *Memory.* The file is held in the page's memory (the pieces, then one `Blob`) until it is saved. A single CSV has no limit of its own but the browser's, so a very large table can fail on a small computer (`EXPORT_TABLES_OUT_OF_MEMORY_MESSAGE` when the browser says so; the time and the memory of a table of about a million rows on a lab PC were not measured). A zip is held to 65,535 entries and 4 GiB (above).
  - *What cannot be interrupted.* One slow `count(*)` or one slow `step()` of a heavy view cannot be interrupted: the engine runs on the page's thread (the plan's section 7), so Cancel and Escape are heard between pieces and between counts only. A count of a view runs the whole view, and every view is counted when the dialog opens, before the student has chosen anything: a view over a three-way self join of 400 rows (9,142,857 rows) held Escape back for some 2 seconds, and nothing in the dialog warns of it. If the student stops the script from Firefox's bar for a script that runs too long, the `finally` does not run and the busy state stays on until the page is reloaded (reasoned, not tried; the import has the same exposure, and the worker of the plan's Phase 7 would cure both).
  - *The browser's part.* The page cannot know whether the browser kept the file: a student who dismisses the box of a browser that asks where to save, or whose browser blocks downloads, still gets the toast and the event, and the event's `file:` line names a file that is not on the disk.
  - *Text.* A text that starts with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@` is written as stored: the CSV is not made safe for a spreadsheet's formulas, which may read such a text as one (Q26). A text that is not valid UTF-8 comes out with U+FFFD for each bad sequence, and a NUL inside a text is written as it is (a program that reads the file may cut the value there). A text that starts with U+FEFF keeps it, in a value, in a column's name and in a table's name (the results pane drops it from a column's name; the file keeps it). A TEXT value `x'deadbeef'` and a BLOB read the same in the CSV (plan Q22).
  - *What it reads.* The export reads through the page's own connection, so it sees what a student's open transaction has changed (and an uncommitted table too), and it changes nothing. No row order is added: `select *` gives the engine's.
  - *The dialog* is for the database that was open when it opened: a different one closes it. The failed table's row stays unticked for the life of the dialog, though a failure could be a passing one; the dialog is opened again to try it.
  - *Checked by hand for this phase*, by the owner on Nov 18, 2026: a real phone, an exported file opened in Excel, and a zip opened by Windows itself. *Not checked by hand yet* (the plan's section 9 lists them all): an exported CSV and a zip opened in Tableau, Safari, a lab PC, `con.csv` on Windows, the dialog with a screen reader, and a table of about a million rows on a lab PC.
- **What a later phase finds.** Phase 8 adds a workbook to the same dialog (the menu item then becomes "Tables…" in both menus: `EXPORT_TABLES_MENU_TEXT` is the one place in the code, and About names no entry). Not all of the export is blind to the format, so this is what it would find, and what it would split, in the order it would do it.
  1. *The loop of `run` writes CSV text into the sink.* The byte-order mark, `headerText`, `rowText` (which also counts the BLOBs for the note) and the UTF-8 encoding are in `run` itself, and a sink takes bytes (`begin`, `write`, `end`, `problem`, `finish`). A workbook is therefore not a second sink behind that interface: it needs the kind and the value of each cell, a sheet at a time, and the limits of a sheet (32,767 characters in a cell, and 1,048,576 rows with the header row, which is 1,048,575 rows of data: the plan's Q25). The step that turns a piece into bytes has to come out of `run` first, so that a format gives its own (for CSV: the mark, the header, the rows to bytes), and the sinks stay the places that keep bytes. The reader (`openRows` gives the kind and the value of every cell) needs no change: a workbook writes number cells for Numbers, text cells for text, leaves a cell out for NULL and for the empty string, and takes an INTEGER beyond 2^53 as a text cell, all from `kinds` and `values`.
  2. *`plan.kind` decides five things that are not about the layout of the output*: which sink `run` makes (`zip` or `file`: `sinkFor`, which throws for any other kind, so a later phase adds its sink there), which message says that the file could not be made (`outputFailure` gives `EXPORT_TABLES_ZIP_FAILED_MESSAGE` or `EXPORT_TABLES_FILE_FAILED_MESSAGE`), whether the event's table line carries the entry's name, whether the toast counts tables or rows, and whether the line beside Export says "N CSV files" (`exportTablesWillSaveText`). A workbook of one table is a file with a named sheet that needs a library, so each of the five wants a condition of its own; the plan of the output is the place to hand them out as fields.
  3. *The format and its words.* The format is fixed in one place and its words in several: the controller's `plan` function passes `format: "csv"` to `planOutput` (the job carries none), and the dialog's title, its line of words and the "CSV files" of the hold line are CSV words. The words of the library's path are zip words too: `EXPORT_TABLES_PROGRESS_LIBRARY` ("Loading the zip tool…"), `EXPORT_TABLES_LIBRARY_FAILED_MESSAGE` and the name `loadZipTool`; a workbook's library needs words of its own, or words that fit both.
  4. *The note and the row's words.* The dialog shows the note of kind `blob` only; a workbook's note for a table left out (its text, or its rows, are beyond a sheet) needs its own wording and place, and "could not be read to its end" is the row's word for any table that failed, which is untrue for a sheet limit.
  5. *What the reader promises and no test holds yet.* The page uses a reader (`export-read.js`) in one way: both budgets given, every value written as text, and no call after the table has ended. Three promises of its header are therefore read and not held by a test (a test never loads a source module into the page, and the bundle shows none of the export's modules): `next()` after `close()` gives no rows and `done`; the value of a NULL is `null`; a budget that is left out is not used. A workbook that leans on one of them holds it with a test of its own.
  What is reused as it is: `planOutput`'s `format` and `library` (the file to fetch and the global it defines), and the field `entries[].name`, but not the way it is made: it comes from `csvFileNames` (a table's name cleaned, cut at 100 characters, `.csv` added), where the plan gives a sheet's name a rule of its own (four steps, cut at 31 characters), so a workbook needs a builder of its own that fills the same field; the dialog's list, counts, hold line, progress, errors and focus; the controller's locks, busy state, library race, Cancel's looks, pace, event (a `table:` line per table) and toast; `exportTablesBlobNote` as the model of a note's wording.
- **Holding an export in a test.** The code has no hook for tests. To keep an export running, a test holds the zip library: it answers `vendor/fflate.min.js` through a gate (`holdVendor`, `tests/helpers.js`), chooses two tables and presses Export; `run` then waits in the library race with the busy state on and the dialog says "Loading the zip tool…", and Cancel is noticed within 100 ms. To stop in the middle of a table, a test slows the page's turns with `slowDown` (`tests/helpers.js`: every rest of the pace costs 15 ms) and exports a table of 6,000 rows of 1,000 characters (about a hundred pieces): the export then takes seconds, and Cancel can be pressed in the middle of the table. To ask for a Run while the dialog is modal, a test presses `#execute` from script, or hands the editor the key (the page behind a modal dialog cannot be pressed by the mouse or the keyboard of a test). A test cannot mount a second export: the module is not one of the twenty-one that the bundle exposes, and a copy of the source module in the page is refused (section 11). What loads in Node is `export-csv.js` and the sinks of `export-tables.js` (`fileSink`, `zipSink`, `sinkFor`), which is where a test gives a zip small limits. A view that counts and then fails: `create view bad as select abs(-9223372036854775807-1) as x union all select 1` (it counts 2 and fails on `integer overflow` when read).

### 9.8 Import of Excel workbooks (assessment plan, Phase 5: flag `excel-import`)

Files: `import-workbook.js` (what is decided before a workbook is read, and the reading itself), `import-zip.js` (a zip's own check), `import-cells.js` (the rules for a sheet's cells) and `import-events.js` (the evidence event of a file), all four pure (no DOM, no storage, no engine; the specs load them in Node, and the library is handed to them, never imported); what Phase 5 added to `import.js`, `import-dialog.js`, `import-db.js`, `import-report.js`, `import-rules.js` and `css/import.css` (no new stylesheet); texts and limits in `config-workflow.js`, put beside their neighbours in the section "Import of CSV files" (the limits and refusals of a workbook after the text limits, the sheets' words after the dialog's, the report's two notes after the report's); the library is `vendor/xlsx.full.min.js` (SheetJS 0.20.3, Apache-2.0, SheetJS LLC: the publisher's own build from its own distribution (not the npm registry's copy, which is an old release), unchanged, 951,904 bytes, its licence in `vendor/xlsx.LICENSE.txt`; it defines the global `XLSX`), fetched by `loadVendor("xlsx.full.min.js", "XLSX")` (`import.js` hands it to the dialog as `excel.loadLibrary`) at the moment the reading of a workbook begins, which is after the question for a large workbook has been answered and after the workbooks before it have been read, and never before: not on a first visit, not for a text file, not when a workbook is merely looked at (`inspectWorkbook` reads no library). The plan's sections 3 and 7 are the specification, and `tests/fixtures/` holds the owner's workbooks (`excel/`, `shopping_db.xlsx`, `impostors/`) with the result each must give (the fixtures' README, `expected/`). The owner has not looked at any of this by hand and has not yet decided anything that the build decided beyond the plan (below): each is theirs to overrule.

The leads below follow a workbook through the code: behind the flag; what a workbook is and what is decided before it is read; the zip's own check; the reading; what the library keeps (and a browser with no memory left); the cells (what a cell gives; dates, times and durations; a number's format; zero-padded formats; numbers and types); the dialog; the run; the events and the report; limits; what a later phase finds; holding a workbook's import in a test. Whoever changes how a date, a time or a duration is read starts at *The cells*: the rules are `serialText`, `isDateFormat` and `formatOf` in `import-cells.js`, held in Node by the lists of formats and dates in `aw5-excel-rules` and, against the owner's expected files, by `aw5-excel-fixtures` (its first-order date test also runs in other time zones). `formatLetters` is shared by `isDateFormat` and `paddingZeros`, so a change to what counts as a date letter changes which formats are reported as zero-padded.

- **Behind the flag.** `flagOn("excel-import")` is read in two places, `mountImport` (`import.js`, once, right after it has found `import` on) and `index.js` (the description under "Open a file…"). `flags.js` already makes the flag off without `import` (`PREVIEW_FLAG_NEEDS`), so a visit with `?preview=-import` has `excel-import` off too, and `mountImport` never reads it when `import` is off. The flag is on by default since Nov 20, 2026; a visit with `-excel-import` and no `-import` is the import of text files alone (9.6), which is the "`import` alone" of this section. With `import` alone nothing differs from 9.6 that a student can see but three things: a workbook is refused with `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_REASON`, the library is never asked for, and the dialog holds none of what this section describes; and the dialog's own code, which the text files' tabs share, has what Phase 5 mended there: a field's message is brought into view with its field and a strip of tabs is no Tab stop (both in *The dialog*), and the `.import-tab` rule has `position: relative`, so that the words hidden inside a tab ("refused", "left out") scroll with the strip, where they were placed at the far end of the tab's line and made the dialog scroll sideways when more files were chosen than fit the strip and one was refused; and `headOf` (9.6), which is not behind the flag, reads on past long white space for a file under a workbook's name, so that with `import` alone a web page saved so is refused as a workbook is, where it was imported as one column of text. With both on, `mountImport` hands the dialog `excel`, `{loadLibrary, inspectWorkbook, readWorkbook}` (null otherwise: every new branch of the dialog is behind `if (excel)`). (`mountImport` returns `run` whenever `import` is on, with or without `excel-import`: the tests call it with a job made by hand.) **Three texts differ from the import alone before any workbook is chosen**, each a constant beside the old one, which stays for `import` alone (tests pin the old words): the dialog's sentence (`IMPORT_DIALOG_TEXT_WITH_SHEETS`: "Each file, or each sheet of a workbook, becomes a table, ..."), the description under "Open a file…" (`OPEN_FILE_WITH_EXCEL_DESCRIPTION`, chosen in `index.js`) and the toast for a choice that mixes files (`IMPORT_MIXED_WITH_EXCEL_MESSAGE`). Nothing else does: a visit that chooses only text files makes the same elements, requests and stored keys with both flags as with `import` alone (`aw0-flags` holds that deeply, `aw5-excel-page` the requests and the library). The chooser, `take` and the drop are as 9.6 has them: `.xlsx` and `.xls` were already the import's names.
- **What a workbook is, and what is decided before it is read** (`inspectWorkbook(file, kind)` -> `{refused: text}` | `{ask: {megabytes, unpackedMegabytes, heavy}, directory}` | `{go: true, directory}`; it never throws). The kind is `kindByStart`'s (9.6): `zip` (an .xlsx, an .xlsm or an .xlsb), `ole2` (an .xls, or a workbook locked with a password) or `markup` (a web page or an XML spreadsheet saved under a workbook's name); the dialog calls this only for those three. For a zip, `zipDirectory` reads the end of the file ("The zip's own check", next) and a directory that is cut short refuses the file as `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_CUT_REASON`, before the library has seen a byte (on some cut files it never returns). A zip with neither `xl/workbook.xml` nor `xl/workbook.bin` among its parts is `IMPORT_NOT_A_WORKBOOK_REASON`, whatever its name says: so is an OpenDocument spreadsheet (its part is `content.xml`), which the library would read but without saying which of its sheets are hidden, so a hidden sheet would be listed as a visible one and ticked. A part's name is looked at as the library looks at it (`partName`: lower case, a slash for a backslash, no slash at the start; some Windows tools write such names and the library reads them), for every test of names. A file that cannot be read at all (changed or taken away after it was chosen) is `IMPORT_UNREADABLE_REASON`. **What the limits measure** (`config-workflow.js`; a megabyte is 1024 x 1024 bytes, as for the text limits; a limit is compared as the text limits are, exactly at it is under it; the sizes said in a text are rounded up, so that a size over a limit is never said as the limit: 500 MB and a byte is "501 MB"). For a zip whose directory was read the measure is the *sheet data*: the unpacked size, as the directory says, of every part the library parses (`holdsSheetData`: a name ending in `.xml` or `.rels`, or in `.bin` outside `xl/embeddings/`, `xl/printersettings/` and `xl/activex/`). Over `EXCEL_WARN_DATA_BYTES` (100 MB) the question is asked first; over `EXCEL_HEAVY_DATA_BYTES` (300 MB) the question has a sentence more (the page may run out of memory); over `EXCEL_MAX_DATA_BYTES` (500 MB) the file is refused (`importWorkbookTooMuchReason(megabytes)`); and so is one whose parts, all of them, unpack to more than `EXCEL_MAX_UNPACKED_BYTES` (1 GB: a picture does not count as sheet data, but the library unpacks every part, so a picture costs memory as large as itself, once, and a zip that compresses a thousand times over costs what it unpacks to). For an `ole2` or `markup` file, and for a zip whose directory the check does not read (Zip64), the measure is the file's size, since an .xls's bytes are its data: `EXCEL_WARN_BYTES` (20 MB) asks, `EXCEL_HEAVY_BYTES` (50 MB) adds the sentence, `EXCEL_MAX_BYTES` (100 MB) refuses (`IMPORT_WORKBOOK_TOO_LARGE_REASON`). Why not the file's size for a zip: a workbook of 20 MB of numbers holds some 100 MB of sheet data and one of 100 MB some 500 MB, while a small file that compresses well is not small to read. `asksFirst(file, directory)` (the measure is over the first limit) is the name of "a workbook that is asked about before it is read", which `listSheets` needs (below). The words of the question are `importLargeWorkbookMessage({megabytes, unpackedMegabytes, heavy})`: the file's size, the sheet data's when the measure is that, the sentence that the page does not answer while the file is read, and for a heavy one the sentence on memory. The answer is the text file's two buttons (`showWarning`, 9.6) with a `proceed` for a workbook: *Read this file* puts it in the queue of readings, *Leave this file out* is a refusal of the student's own.
- **The zip's own check** (`import-zip.js`). Why it is there: of 300 copies of a workbook with one bit changed the library stopped for good on 17 (a page that never answers again), read 60 differently with no error (a sheet shorter, a cell changed), threw on 16 and read 207 as before; and of 120 copies cut short it stopped on 6. A zip keeps a checksum and a size for every part and the browser can inflate a part, so the page can tell a sound zip from a damaged one before the library runs. `zipDirectory(file)` reads the last 64 KB and 22 bytes for the end record, then the central directory from where the end record says it starts (a second slice when it is longer than that tail), and resolves `{entries: [{name, method, flags, crc, packed, unpacked, at}], unpacked}`, or `{damaged: true}` for no end record, a directory out of place or cut, an entry that is no entry, an archive in several parts, or a count that disagrees with the end record, or `null` for a Zip64 file (a marker in an entry's size or start with the Zip64 extra field of that entry, or in the end record with the Zip64 locator before it: nothing is checked then, the library's own throw decides, and the file's size is the measure). A marker with nothing of Zip64 beside it is `{damaged: true}`: the library takes such a size at its word and runs out of memory. `checkZip(bytes, entries, {rest})` is true when every part is sound: its local header is where the directory says and has the method the directory says, the part is stored (method 0) or deflated (method 8), and it unpacks (`DecompressionStream("deflate-raw")`, read piece by piece, never held whole) to exactly the stored size with the stored CRC-32. A part is left unpacking as soon as it has given more bytes than the directory says (a zip bomb costs what its directory claims). A part that is encrypted or of another method is left to the library (no verdict). `rest()` is awaited between parts and after every 4 MB, so the page can draw and a press on Cancel is heard. The reader of the stream is cancelled whenever the check stops (a `finally`), so that no rejection is left without a taker. Where the browser has no `DecompressionStream` for `deflate-raw`, nothing is checked and the library reads the file as it is (`canUnpack`). **What it cannot see**: a part that is wrong inside a sound zip (a file saved by a program that wrote a bad sheet and a good checksum), and anything in an .xls or a web page, which have no checksums. It also refuses a workbook any of whose parts fails, also a part the import would not have used. Run once over every one-bit copy of one workbook (162,920 of them) with this check in front of the library, none hung and none was read differently; `aw5-excel-rules` holds a seeded sample of 300 copies, each copy the check passes read in a worker thread under a time limit and giving the original's cells or a refusal as damaged, and no copy the check refuses is ever handed to the library.
- **The reading** (`readWorkbook(file, kind, {directory, loadLibrary, rest, frame, current})` -> `{sheets, charts}` | `{refused: text}` | `{moved: true}`). `rest` is the pace's, `frame` is `nextFrame`, `current()` says whether the dialog's session is still the one that began the reading: whenever it is false, or `rest` throws, the reading stops with `{moved: true}` and the dialog does nothing (a thrown `rest` is never a verdict on the file; the one exception is a `rest` that fails for want of memory while the dialog is still there, below). In order: the library (`loadLibrary`; a rejection is `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_LIBRARY_FAILED_REASON` for this tab only, nothing is remembered of it since the loader forgets a failed attempt, and the other tabs go on); the file's bytes (`new Uint8Array(await file.arrayBuffer())`; a read that fails is `IMPORT_UNREADABLE_REASON`, or the words for no memory when what was thrown says so); the zip's directory when the caller gave none (`directory` undefined: so a zip is never handed to the library unchecked by a caller that forgot to inspect it; `null` is "no check for this one"); the zip check (a failure is `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_DAMAGED_REASON`, and the library never sees the file); **a frame** (`await frame()`, so that the tab's "Reading the file…" is painted before the page stops answering); and then **the one call that cannot be interrupted**: `XLSX.read(bytes, {type: "array", dense: true, cellNF: true, sheetStubs: true, cellHTML: false})`, with the page frozen meanwhile (no progress, no Cancel, no Escape: the plan's section 7 says so, and the question for a large workbook tells the student). Every call gets a new options object, since the library writes into the one it is given and a second read with it throws. `dense` is for memory (the same cells in less of it; the other mode stops making progress above about eight million cells in a sheet) and gives the cells as `ws["!data"][row][column]`; `cellNF` gives each number cell its format; `sheetStubs` gives cells that only have formatting (so that a formula with no stored value shows); `cellText` stays on (`cell.w`, what Excel showed) and `cellDates` off (a date is a serial number: the app's own calendar turns it into text). **A web page or an XML spreadsheet is decoded by the app first** (`decodePage`: the byte-order mark's encoding, else UTF-8 when every byte fits, else Windows-1252, strictly, as for a text file), and the library gets text, `XLSX.read(text, {type: "string", raw: true, dense: true, ...})`: left alone it reads the bytes of a page as UTF-8 whatever the page says, and throws on an XML spreadsheet that declares another encoding. A web page is a `markup` file that comes back with `wb.Workbook === undefined` (only it has the library's invented sheet names, which are never shown: its tables are called "Table 1", "Table 2", `importPageTableLabel`, and their table names come from the file's name, `tableNameFor`, made unique like any other); an XML spreadsheet has its own sheet names. A page with no table makes the library throw. **What a thrown value means** (`reasonForThrown`; the library throws Errors, TypeErrors and plain strings, so it is looked at as `String(error?.message ?? error)` and its words are never shown): `/password/i` is a locked workbook (`IMPORT_WORKBOOK_LOCKED_REASON`: an .xlsx locked with a password is an OLE2 file); the engines' words for having no memory are `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_NO_MEMORY_REASON` (below); anything else is `IMPORT_PAGE_UNREADABLE_REASON` for `markup` and `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_UNREADABLE_REASON` for the rest. **The sheets** (`listSheets`): for each name in `wb.SheetNames`, in order, the sheet object is `sheetNamed(wb.Sheets, name)`: the library keeps sheets in a plain object, so a sheet called `constructor` must not find the object's own (`Object.hasOwn`), and for one called `__proto__` the library's assignment set the object's prototype, which is then the sheet. A sheet whose object has a `!type` (a chart, a macro, a dialog sheet) is not listed, and `charts` counts them (the dialog says "Chart sheets are not listed."). A sheet's state is the entry of `wb.Workbook.Sheets` with its name (the first entry of a name counts), `Hidden` 1 hidden or 2 very hidden (both are `hidden` and both are listed, not ticked), anything else visible. A workbook that lists no sheet at all (every one a chart) is `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_NO_SHEETS_REASON`; one whose listed sheets all hold nothing, or are too wide, or damaged, is listed as usual, each sheet saying why it cannot be ticked, and the dialog then offers only Close. A name with no sheet object makes the workbook damaged, or lost (below). The sheets are made by `readSheet` ("The cells", below), one `pause` (a rest, with the watch put by) after each. The bytes are let go after the read (the sheets are the library's own objects; nothing is copied), the workbook is kept by the dialog while it is open and let go when the session ends, and nothing is read twice: what is imported is what the dialog showed (a workbook changed in Excel after it was chosen is imported as shown; a text file in that case is refused, 9.6), so a workbook needs no mark (`markOf`). **Two kinds of file that are no ordinary .xlsx or .xls.** An OpenDocument spreadsheet is refused before the reading (above). A binary workbook (.xlsb) under an .xlsx name is a zip with `xl/workbook.bin` and is read as an .xlsx is (every .xlsx fixture written again as .xlsb by the library gives the same sheets and tables); a macro workbook (.xlsm) is an .xlsx to the reader; the file chooser has no `accept` list, and the names the import reads are the plan's five (`IMPORT_NAME`: .csv, .tsv, .txt, .xlsx, .xls). A file ending in `.xlsm`, `.xlsb` or `.ods` is the import's too (`OTHER_SPREADSHEET_NAME`) and is refused in its tab in plain words, by its name, before a byte of it is read (9.6); with `import` off it is tried as a database, as before. Reading an `.xlsm` or an `.xlsb` by its name is the owner's to ask for.
- **What the library keeps of a workbook, and a workbook that the browser has no memory left for** (`import-workbook.js`: `letStringsGo`, `formatsHeld`, `forWantOfMemory`, `watchCall`, `watchStep`, `roomAgain`, `readWorkbook` and `reading`; `import-dialog.js`: `readSheets` and `buildUnit`). This is the part most likely to be broken without being seen, and the specs that hold it are in `aw5-excel-rules` ("what the library keeps of a workbook", "a workbook that the browser has no memory left for") and `aw5-excel-limits` (its last group); read their comments before changing any of it.
  - *What the library keeps.* The library holds the shared strings of the last .xlsx (or .xlsb) it read in a variable of its own: every text of the workbook, still in memory when the workbook has long been let go (the dialog cancelled, the import done), until another .xlsx is read or the page is loaded again. Measured: 456 MB of the page's memory after Cancel in Chromium for a workbook of 1.4 million text cells (a file of 14 MB, read without a question); 10 to 11 MB once the page lets them go, whatever the workbook's size. An .xls and a web page leave nothing. So after the library's read of a zip, whether it gave a workbook or threw (not when it threw for want of memory: the watch does it then, below), `letStringsGo(XLSX)` gives it one more workbook to read, a small one that holds nothing (written once by the library's own writer, kept for that library object in a `WeakMap`, and given a table of number formats of its own that is empty: the writer would put the library's present table into a book that has none, and a workbook with very large formats would be held in those bytes, and in the library's table whenever they are read, for as long as the page lives), with `{type: "array"}` alone, and then asks the library for its table of formats once more (`XLSX.SSF.get_table()`: the library keeps the last table it was asked for in a field of its own, `SSF._table`, and that was the workbook's, which the sheets' copy is made from; left there it would hold the workbook's formats until the next workbook is read); what goes wrong in it is swallowed, unless `strict` is given, which lets a failure for want of memory through. The specs tell this read from the read of a chosen file by its options (a chosen file's carries `dense: true`; this one has `type` and nothing else): see the last bullet.
  - *The table of number formats.* The library makes its table of number formats (`XLSX.SSF`) anew at every read, the read of the workbook that holds nothing included, while a sheet's cells are worked out later (a preview when its tab is shown, the table at Import; `formatOf` in `import-cells.js` looks in that table for a cell the library gave no format). So `formatsHeld(XLSX)` hands each workbook's sheets a copy of the table as their own read left it (`{get_table, format}` with the library's formatter, which takes the options as the library's does), in the place of `XLSX.SSF` itself, which by then is another workbook's. The search compares the shown text it finds with a text made with the workbook's own date system (`{date1904}` is passed to the formatter): the library made the text with it, and a 1904 workbook's East Asian date format (the built-in ids 27 to 31, 36, 50, 57 and 58 have no format text in the library's table) would be found no format at all with the 1900 system, and the cell come out as a plain number.
  - *What a browser does with no memory left, and why the steps are watched from outside.* When the browser has no memory for a workbook the library's call does throw ("out of memory"; Chromium says "Invalid string length" or "Array buffer allocation failed" for a text or a buffer it cannot make), but the memory is still full then (the library holds what it had read), so whatever makes an object fails in its turn. In Firefox, with a workbook of seven million texts: the `catch` began and could not work out its reason, a `finally` ran, the reading's promise was rejected, the dialog could not make its line of refusal, and the tab said "Reading the file…" for ever with the page awake behind it. In another case the library's call returns with the last of the memory and the page's next step fails instead. So **a catch that cannot finish is not a thing to depend on**, and the steps are watched from outside them: `watchCall(onLost, {every})` sets a timer (`setInterval`, `WORKBOOK_WATCH_MS` = 250 ms) just before a step that does not wait for anything, and takes it away **only when the step has ended with its outcome in hand: never in a `finally` (which runs for a step that was lost as well) and never across a wait** (a timer that is set while the page waits would fire at once and tell of a step that is only slow). A timer cannot fire while a call is running, so one that fires with its step not ended tells that the step was lost. It comes round again every `every` ms until the telling has returned without throwing: a turn in which the telling itself fails (for want of memory) changes nothing, and the next one tries again, whatever it throws (looking at what was thrown would take memory too). The consequence for any telling: it must not throw for any other reason, or it would be tried for ever, and what waits for it would wait for ever.
  - *What the watch does before it tells.* In `readWorkbook` the watch's turn first lets the library's strings go (`letStringsGo` with `strict`, for a zip: while even that cannot be done it throws and the next turn tries again), then asks for room (`roomAgain()`: it makes 200,000 small objects, some megabytes, and throws while the browser cannot: with less room than that, the next thing a student does would fail as the lost step did), and only then resolves with `{refused: IMPORT_WORKBOOK_NO_MEMORY_REASON}`: the file is not said to be refused before the browser has cleared up. `readWorkbook` is a wrapper that resolves from whichever speaks first, the reading (`reading`, the old body) or the watch, and drops a rejection of the reading while the watch is set (`watch.begun()`). In `reading` the watch is set (`watch.begin(XLSX)`) from just before the library's call until the sheets are listed, over every stretch in which the page's own script runs, and taken away for each wait (`pause`, between two sheets) and at every outcome. What is thrown for want of memory (`forWantOfMemory`: `/out of memory|allocation size overflow|array buffer allocation failed|invalid string length/i`, the engines' words, read from the message or from the bare thing thrown) leaves the watch set; any other failure takes it away and is a fault, thrown as it was. A turn that the page gives itself between two sheets (`calm`, which turns a thrown `rest` into "the dialog has moved on") and that fails for want of memory while the dialog is still there is a loss too and not the dialog having moved on; `pause` sets the watch again for it. A browser may also end the script outright: then no catch and no `finally` runs at all, and the watch is all there is.
  - *The dialog's own step.* After a reading, from the answer to what the tab shows, the dialog's step (`readSheets`: refuse the tab, or `showSheets`) runs under the same watch through `watchStep(step, onLost)`: a promise that resolves with what the step gave, rejects with a fault that is not about memory, and for a loss resolves only when `onLost` has returned (tried at every turn until it does). Nothing in it depends on a catch that can finish: a catch that fails in its turn is one more rejection while the watch is set, and is dropped. So the turn that reads the workbooks (they are read one after the other through `workbookTurn`) waits for the telling and hears of no failure, and **the files chosen behind a lost workbook are still read**, when there is room for them: a throw here would end the loop and their tabs would say for ever that they are being read. The dialog's `onLost` first lets go what fills the memory, asking nothing of the browser for it (the answer, the tab's workbook, and what the tab has shown of its sheets so far; until there is room again the tab is one that is still being read, with nothing to show: Import is off for it as for any file being read, which `onLost` sets itself before anything else, since the step can fail after the sheets were first shown, when Import was on, and nothing else turns it off before there is room, and sets only while the dialog is still on these files, since the button is the dialog's and files chosen since have their own say over it; and the CSV file behind it waits), then asks for room (`roomAgain()`), then puts the tab back to its name alone and refuses it. **The words a student reads**: the file's own reason where the reading had refused it and only the refusal could not be shown (`lostReason = read.refused ?? IMPORT_WORKBOOK_NO_MEMORY_REASON`: a password stays a password), else the words for no memory. The same words are said for a file whose whole bytes the browser has no memory to hold (the first thing a workbook asks of it), and for Chromium's cap on the length of one text. A sheet's panel counts as built only when it was made whole (`buildUnit`: `unit.built` is set last): one whose preview failed for want of memory when its tab was first shown is made again the next time the sheet is shown, and the workbook is not refused for it.
  - *A sheet the library leaves out.* The library swallows whatever the reading of a sheet throws, and of the shared texts, and goes on (`catch (e) { if (opts.WTF) throw e; }` in its reader of a zip): the workbook comes back with the sheet's name listed and no sheet to it. That is what it does for a sheet whose part is damaged, and just the same when the browser had no memory for the sheet. As a rule the library then fails again at once, the memory still being full, and the watch tells of it; but in one run of six in real Firefox, with a workbook of seven million texts, it came back one sheet short and the workbook was called "damaged". Nothing the page can reach says which it was: the exception is gone, and the option `WTF` that would pass it on also makes the library throw on every oddity of a sound file, so it is not used. So `listSheets` goes by size: a workbook that is asked about before it is read (`asksFirst`) and comes back with a name that has no sheet is refused with `IMPORT_WORKBOOK_SHEET_LOST_REASON`, whose words say both causes and name the size as the likely one; a smaller one is damaged (`IMPORT_WORKBOOK_DAMAGED_REASON`), as before, since no browser lacks the memory for it. The line is a guess: on a machine with very little memory a smaller workbook could be told wrongly that it is damaged. A workbook is never imported with a sheet short: any name with no sheet refuses the whole of it. (A shared-texts part that the library gave up on leaves it with no texts at all; a sheet with a cell that points at a text then throws in its turn and is left out, so that case ends here too, and never as a table of blanks.)
  - *What is not guarded.* Running out of memory during the import itself (the page stays busy until it is loaded again); a failure for want of memory in the small things the page does before the library's call (the zip's directory, the zip check, the wait for a frame, the fetch of the library), which is told as "could not read this file" or, where the page's own catch cannot run, not at all; a browser that never has room again (the reading's watch then never tells, and every later workbook waits behind it until the page is loaded again; text files are not held up); a Chromium tab that crashes outright ("Aw, Snap"); a library that swallows a failure for want of memory twice in one cell (it keeps the cell's number format and its shown text in two separate steps and swallows what either throws), which would leave a date as its serial number with the workbook whole (a reading of the library's code, never seen to happen); and a student who answers the browser's own "this page is slow" prompt with Stop, who is then told that the browser ran out of memory (the words say what did not happen; no browser here can be made to show that prompt, so it is untried).
- **The cells** (`import-cells.js`; its header is the reference for the exports). A sheet is what the dialog lists and a table is made from: `readSheet(ws, {name, label, hidden, page, date1904, ssf})` -> `{name, label, hidden: 0 | 1 | 2, state, rows, columns, start, page, ...}` and what the rules keep in it (the worksheet, its rows, the rectangle, the merged blocks, the date system and the formatter: nobody outside reaches those, see `previewRows`, `tableOf`, `storedRow` below). *The rectangle*: the smallest that holds every cell with a value, worked out after the merged cells are blanked (every cell of a merged block but its top left is treated as empty: the library keeps values that Excel hides there; the blocks are kept as a list with an index by first row, never expanded, so a block that covers a million rows costs what a block costs). A value is a value by `holdsValue`: a stub, an empty text, a formula with no stored value and a merged-away cell hold none; a cell of spaces holds one. `rows` and `columns` are the rectangle's size, `start` its first cell's address ("C5"); rows, and the box "Column names are on row", count from the rectangle's first row. The sheet's own range (`!ref`) decides nothing, not even that a sheet is empty: it covers cells that only have formatting, and a sound sheet that holds no cell can declare a larger range. State: `ready`; `empty` (no value: listed, cannot be ticked); `wide` (the rectangle has more than `MAX_COLUMNS`, 2,000, columns); `damaged` (a cell past Excel's own bounds, a row after the 1,048,576th or a column after the 16,384th, which no workbook holds and which the library gives from a few bytes of file; a cell in the last row or the last column is no fault). Cells are reached through `ws["!data"][r]?.[c]`, and **a row is walked by the places that hold a cell (its own keys), never by its length**: the library gives a list of billions for a cell far away, and a row with a few cells and one far to the right is a sparse list in which every place looked at by its number costs a search (100,000 such rows took 30 seconds by number and take some tens of milliseconds by key). A row with no cell is a hole, a row of the rectangle with no cell is a row of NULLs, and the last row is the last one that holds a cell, not the list's length. Never `Math.min(...cells)`: a spread of a million cells overflows the stack.
  - *What a cell gives* (`cellValue`; the importer reads and does not clean: a cell is what it holds, not what Excel showed). Text: as it is, spaces kept; empty text is NULL. A number: its number, whatever its format shows, except the date, time and duration formats below. `TRUE` and `FALSE`: the words. An error: its text (`#N/A`, `#DIV/0!`: `cell.w`, else from the code in `cell.v`; the library exports no table of them, so the module holds eight). A formula: its last stored value by these rules; with none (an .xlsx: a stub, `t: "z"` with `f`; an .xls: an empty text with `f`) NULL; a boolean written by LibreOffice carries `f: "true"` and has a value. Booleans, errors and dates are text in the table. A number the library could not read or hold comes as NaN or Infinity with an error's text in `w`: that text.
  - *Dates, times, durations.* A number is a date or time when its number format is a date or time format, and is stored as text, **by value**, in the plan's order (`serialText`): the seconds are rounded to the nearest, once, first (`Math.round(v * 86400)`: a time of 09:05:00 is stored as 0.378472222222222 and its seconds are 32699.99999999998); a **duration** (the format's letters hold a bracketed part, `[h]`, `[mm]`, `[ss]`) is `H:MM:SS` with the hours not padded and free to pass 24; a **time**, when the stored value is below one day, is `HH:MM:SS` (one that rounds up to a whole day is `00:00:00`, as Excel shows it); a **date** otherwise is `YYYY-MM-DD`, with ` HH:MM:SS` after it when the seconds left over are not zero. By value means whatever the format shows: 0 under `yyyy-mm-dd` is `00:00:00` in both date systems, and 1.5 under `hh:mm:ss` is `1900-01-01 12:00:00` (the plan's order, and known). A number that no date can be (negative, past 9999-12-31, a duration below zero or beyond what a number holds exactly) is imported as the plain number it is. *The calendar is the module's own*, in whole days, with no `Date`, no time zone and no `XLSX.SSF.parse_date_code` (a day wrong in time zones that skipped a day, seconds cut off, and nothing for the last half day of the range): the 1900 system has serial 60 as `1900-02-29`, which never existed, as in Excel, 1 to 59 are 1 January to 28 February 1900 and from 61 on the day is `serial - 25569` days after 1970-01-01; the 1904 system (`wb.Workbook.WBProps.date1904`) is `serial + 1462 - 25569`; the last day is 9999-12-31 (serial 2958465, or 2957003 in 1904). `aw5-excel-rules` runs the whole reading in child processes with other time zones.
  - *A number's format.* `isDateFormat` is the app's own test, not the library's `is_date` (which calls `0.00e+00` a date): the format's *letters* (its first section, up to the first `;` outside quotes, without quoted text, without the character after a backslash, an underscore or an asterisk, and without bracketed parts but those that are all one of `h`, `m`, `s` in either case) hold one of `d m h y s` in either case; `General` and `@` are not. The library leaves `cell.z` undefined on the first cell of each built-in format outside its table of 29 (the East Asian date formats among them), and its own `is_date(undefined)` throws: such a cell's format is the first format the library now holds (`get_table()`, the copy `formatsHeld` made) whose `format(format, cell.v)` gives exactly `cell.w`, date formats first, else `General`; the search is made once for a cell, over a list made once for a sheet, for no more than 1,000 cells of a sheet (the later ones are `General`: a workbook has one such cell for each built-in format it uses), and a format that the library cannot show is not the one. What is worked out about a format is kept for each distinct format of a sheet (and emptied when it reaches 5,000: formats come from the file).
  - *Zero-padded formats* (`paddingZeros`, for the report only; the table never holds the zeros). A format pads with zeros when it is no date or time format, its letters hold no `/`, `E` or `e`, and they have two or more `0` before any decimal point (`00000`, `000-000`, `0000.00`; not `0.00`, not `#,##0`, not `0 0/0`). A column is named, in the report, only when one of its data cells is a number, has such a format, and has fewer digits before its point than the format has zeros (that cell lost zeros): the example is the first such cell's text as Excel showed it (`cell.w`, else `format(z, v)` inside a `try`, else none: the column is then named with no example).
  - *Numbers and types.* `numberInFull(v)` writes a number out in full: the shortest digits that give the number back, with the exponent written out, no trailing zeros, no bare point, `-0` as `0` (`1234.5`, `0.0000001`, `12345678901234567000`, `602000000000000000000000`). It is used where a numeric cell lands in a TEXT column, where a header cell holds a number, and in the preview. A column's type is decided as for a text file, by `import-rules.js`: a text cell goes through `tallyValue` as a CSV field does (`00123` stays text, `42` is a whole number); **a numeric cell goes through `tallyNumber`** (added to `import-rules.js` for this), which moves the same counters (`whole` when `Number.isInteger(v) && Math.abs(v) < 1e15`, strictly, else `decimal`) without ever judging a number by its written text (6.02e23 written out has 24 digits). So `columnType` and `blockingValue` serve both. Stored values (`storedRow`): an INTEGER column holds a numeric cell as its number (no negative zero) and a text cell as its text (the column converts it); a REAL column the same (`import-db.js` binds a number for a REAL column as a double, below); a TEXT column a numeric cell as `numberInFull(v)` and anything else as its text; NULL stays null.
  - *Where a sheet's cells are reached.* Only through `previewRows(sheet, from, count)` (the dialog's preview: texts, null for NULL), `tableOf(sheet, {hasNames, namesRow, rest})` (a table: `{rows, columns: [{name, original, type, blanks, blocking}], emptyFormulas, padded, ...}`, worked out in steps of `IMPORT_SHEET_CELLS` cells with `rest()` awaited between two) and `storedRow(table, index)`, each from one place, and a sheet's table name through `sheetTableName(sheet, fileName)`: `sanitiseName(label)`, or `FALLBACK_TABLE_NAME` when nothing is left (never `tableNameFor`, which would cut `Q1.5` at the dot), and for a web page the file's name. With "has a row of names" on and the names on row `n` (of the rectangle) the names are that row's values through `columnNames` (a number by `numberInFull`, a date by its ISO text, a blank cell `column_<its position in the rectangle>`), the rows above it are left out and the rows below are the data; with it off, as for a text file, the columns are `column_1` up to the width and every row of the rectangle is data whatever the row box holds. `tableOf` throws a `RangeError` for a sheet that is not ready or a names row that is not a row of it: the dialog decides those before it asks. `emptyFormulas` counts every formula cell with no value on the sheet, below the names row (the whole sheet without a row of names), inside the table's columns or beyond them.
- **The dialog** (`import-dialog.js`, `css/import.css`). *Units.* A text tab and a sheet are both **units** (`isSheet`): each has a table name, a row of names, a preview and a tick (a text file is always ticked). `nameUnits()` is what takes a name (every text tab that is not refused, a file whose kind is not known yet counting as one, and every sheet of a workbook that has been read in state `ready`, ticked or not), `tickedUnits()` what gives a table, `holds()` what holds Import up. A workbook's tab is the file through every step before its sheets are known (reading, the question, a refusal, left out); when it has been read its panel holds the sheets. *Strip and ticks.* A workbook that lists two or more sheets gets a second strip of tabs inside its panel (`role="tablist"`, `aria-label` "Sheets of <file>", `importSheetsLabel`), one tab and one panel for each sheet, ids `${tab.id}-s${i}-tab`, `-panel`; the strip and its panels are put into the document first and only then is `mountTabs` called (it looks panels up in the document, and its first call runs `onShow`, which builds the first sheet's panel). One sheet listed: no second strip and no buttons, the panel shows that sheet and its own tick box is the control (the line with the count still appears when chart sheets were left out, so that the student is told that they are not listed). Above the strip a line with `role="status"` ("3 of 4 sheets will be imported.", with " 1 more cannot be imported." and " Chart sheets are not listed." as they apply: `importSheetsCountText`) is written only when its words change ("No sheet of this workbook can be imported." when none can be ticked), and beside it the two buttons "Tick every sheet" and "Untick every sheet" (every sheet that can be ticked, hidden ones included). The first sheet that is ticked is selected at first, else the first listed. A **sheet that cannot be ticked** (`empty`, `wide`, `damaged`) has the quiet sign and words for a screen reader in its tab; in its panel the tick box has `aria-disabled="true"` (not `disabled`: it keeps its place in the Tab order and its reason is read, `aria-describedby`), is never checked, and its `click` is prevented and Enter in it does nothing (`aria-disabled` alone does not stop a tick box; in any usable field, Enter presses Import as before). A **hidden** sheet (hidden or very hidden) is listed, not ticked, with the word and the note `IMPORT_SHEET_HIDDEN_NOTE`, shown only when it can be ticked.
